================================================================================
#109 PHASED SOAK — 16 HOURS, THREE TRAFFIC RATES, LEAK VERSUS CACHE
================================================================================

#105 (docs/acceptance/soak.txt) returned the middle verdict — rss=POSITIVE BUT
BELOW THRESHOLD, warmed OLS +1.2334 MiB/min, CI [+1.1161, +1.3508], Theil-Sen
+1.2900 — and then spent its §7 explaining that it could not say WHY, because
over its warmed window `rows ~ elapsed` had Pearson r = 1.000000. "Leaks per
minute" and "caches per row" were one claim wearing two hats.

This run was designed, and pre-registered, to break that. It did break it. The
answer it returns is not the one the design was shaped to detect, and that is
said plainly below rather than smoothed: the leak term L is INDISTINGUISHABLE
FROM ZERO, the cache term c is INDISTINGUISHABLE FROM ZERO, and the
pre-registered primary verdict is leakVsCache=NEITHER. The fleet did not
meaningfully grow over sixteen hours at any of the three rates. The +1.2334
MiB/min #105 measured did not reproduce at #105's own rate.

Two things went wrong with the instruments and both are reported before the
result, not after it: three of the five eyes stopped executing their poll loop
inside the first ten minutes and stayed parked for the remaining ~15.8 hours,
and the sampler lost 380 of a planned 1,920 samples to load excursions. Neither
is fatal to the pre-registered verdict; both bound what it can be taken to mean.
See §10.

   (end)             960        2026-08-28T07:16:09Z  (warm-up's 105 samples are DISCARDED WHOLE and appear here only for the
  record; "analysed samples" for phases 1-12 means past that phase's own
  15-minute settling allowance, per phases.ts isAnalysed().)

  THE MANIPULATION WORKED. That is the first thing to check and it is the
  precondition for everything else. Observed rows/min per analysed phase
  against the three pre-registered nominal rates (Z=0, A=60, B=600):

    Z phases  3, 4, 8, 10 :  0.0, 0.0, 0.0, 0.0 rows/min — EXACTLY frozen.
                             rows was byte-identical at the first and last
                             analysed sample of all four.
    A phases  1, 5, 9, 12 :  13.9, 37.5, 58.2, 59.8 rows/min
    B phases  2, 6, 7, 11 :  595.2, 598.0, 550.3, 597.6 rows/min

  A phases 9 and 12 hit the nominal 60 to within 3%. Phases 1 and 5 fell short
  (13.9 and 37.5) because the traffic writer was competing with load1 medians of
  120.3 and, earlier in that window, 285-317. That is a real deviation and it is
  carried into P4 rather than hidden: the per-row statistic uses each phase's
  OBSERVED rows, not its nominal rate.

  Collinearity, the whole reason this design exists:

    #105 warmed window   rows ~ elapsed  r = 1.000000
    #109 analysed window rows ~ elapsed  r = 0.978756

  0.9788 is still high — a monotone counter against a monotone clock always will
  be — but the design does not rest on the pooled correlation. It rests on the
  fact that inside four separate 60-minute windows, spread across the run, rows
  did not move AT ALL while elapsed did. That is a genuine break, and it is what
  makes P1, P2 and P3 answerable.

  Two other correlations, both new and both worth stating:

    totalRss ~ elapsed  r = +0.0629   (#105: +0.7687)
    totalRss ~ rows     r = +0.0805   (#105: +0.7688)
    totalRss ~ load1    r = -0.2212   (#105: -0.4005)

  #105's fleet RSS tracked its clock at r = 0.77. This one does not track
  anything at r > 0.09. That is the headline result restated as a correlation,
  before any model is fitted to it.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
2. THE RAW SERIES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

  §12 carries all 1540 samples as a tab-separated reduction of series.jsonl. The
  reduction is lossless for every quantity any number in this file is computed
  from. Precisely what it keeps and what it drops:

    KEPT  elapsedMinutes (3 dp, as written), phase label, phase rate, rows,
          totalRssMiB, per-eye RSS in MiB for all five eyes, load1, walBytes,
          shmBytes, archiveBytes, sqlite.pageCount, all three searchMs values,
          restartLines.
    DROPPED  `at` (an ISO timestamp exactly reconstructible as
          2026-08-27T15:15:58.434Z + elapsedMinutes to within the sampler's own
          jitter), the five per-eye pids (constant all run: archiver 37276,
          digest 37278, honcho 37277, retro 37279, reviewer 37275 — and their
          constancy IS the restart accounting, §8), eyeCount (5 on every sample
          but the first), missingRss (0 on every sample), and the four constant
          sqlite fields cacheSize=2000, pageSize=4096, freelistCount (0 or 1),
          plus pageCacheBoundBytes which is min(cacheSize,pageCount)*pageSize
          and was 8,192,000 on every sample from minute 8 onward.

  The original is recoverable and verifiable:
    sha256(series.jsonl) = 37a8664e68a5f257cb59f45932f074b9c0861483eefe2fa9a707a938c7518485
    at /private/tmp/argus-soak-5oAcZD/series.jsonl, left in place untouched.

  memprobe.jsonl (3,209 readings, 640 KB) is NOT reproduced here. It is
  summarised completely in §6 P5, and its distribution across eyes is so lopsided
  (1588 / 1587 / 20 / 10 / 4) that the summary loses nothing. Its checksum is in
  §1.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
3. THE PRE-REGISTERED VERDICTS, VERBATIM
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

  Both files were committed in c9175e0 and neither has been touched since; `git
  status` is clean on both. `analyze109.ts` re-implements #105's machinery rather
  than importing `analyze.ts`, so that #105's own output stays byte-reproducible;
  `latency109.ts` DOES import `latency.ts` and hands #110's verdict back
  untouched under `preRegistered`.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
4. READING THE PRIMARY VERDICT
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

  verdict = rss=POSITIVE BUT BELOW THRESHOLD
            restarts=NONE
            leakVsCache=NEITHER
            cause=ARENA / UNRETURNED PAGES

  rss=POSITIVE BUT BELOW THRESHOLD is nominally the SAME string #105 returned,
  and that similarity is misleading enough to be worth killing immediately:

    #105 warmed OLS   +1.2334 MiB/min  CI [+1.1161, +1.3508]  Theil-Sen +1.2900
    #109 analysed OLS +0.0129 MiB/min  CI [+0.0009, +0.0249]  Theil-Sen +0.0157

  #109's slope is 96 times smaller. Its CI excludes zero only just — the lower
  bound is +0.0009 MiB/min, which is 0.9 KiB per minute, or 830 KiB over the
  whole sixteen hours across five processes. It clears "excludes zero" and it
  clears nothing else. Extrapolated the way #105's §7 extrapolated: at
  +0.0129 MiB/min the fleet reaches 512 MiB from its final 343.69 MiB in 218
  hours, i.e. nine days, and at the CI lower bound, 8.6 YEARS. #105's own
  per-eye extrapolations landed between 7.5 h and 100 h. This is a different
  regime, not a smaller reading of the same one.

  Fleet RSS at the end was 343.69 MiB; the peak, 393.64 MiB, occurred at
  2026-08-27T15:17:29Z — ninety seconds in, during the start-up transient, and
  never again in sixteen hours. #105 saw the same shape (491.7 MiB at 0.50 min,
  falling to ~143 by minute 20), which is the whole reason the warm-up exists.

  leakVsCache=NEITHER is the primary verdict, and it means what the
  pre-registration says it means: both CIs contain zero.

    byFloor (the pre-registered decomposition, 5-minute buckets, 12 phases)
      L  = -0.1634 MiB/min   CI [-0.6640, +0.3372]    Theil-Sen L = -0.0832
      c  = +0.002502 MiB/min per row-per-sample
                             CI [-0.000373, +0.005378]  Theil-Sen c = +0.001901
      c in the reportable unit: +1.2512 MiB per 1,000 rows

    byMean (the same fit on mean rather than floor, reported so that
            "whichever reads better" is unavailable)
      L  = -0.0667 MiB/min   CI [-0.7216, +0.5882]    Theil-Sen L = +0.1094
      c  = +0.002093           CI [-0.001669, +0.005855]  Theil-Sen c = +0.001372
      c = +1.0465 MiB per 1,000 rows

  The floor and mean fits agree in sign on c (+) and DISAGREE in sign on L
  (-0.1634 floor OLS, -0.0667 mean OLS, but Theil-Sen mean +0.1094). The
  pre-registered DISAGREEMENT clause is written on OLS-versus-Theil-Sen sign and
  on floor-versus-mean sign; judge() did not fire it, and I am not going to
  overrule its own rule from the outside. What is true and worth saying is that
  every one of these four L estimates is a small number whose CI spans zero in
  both directions: they do not disagree about a leak, they agree there is no
  signal to have a sign.

  Model-free pooled floor slope by rate, four replicates each, mean ± sd:

      Z   -0.2480  sd 0.7055   CI [-1.3704, +0.8745]
      A   +0.0056  sd 0.3533   CI [-0.5565, +0.5676]
      B   +0.5779  sd 0.7544   CI [-0.6223, +1.7781]

  All three CIs contain zero. The B mean is ten times the A mean and A is
  ~zero and Z is negative — that ORDERING is the cache-shaped one, and it is
  the only cache-shaped thing in the file. It does not survive its own error
  bars: sd 0.75 on four replicates against a mean of 0.58.

  The between-replicate spread is the thing four replicates were bought for, and
  it is the finding. Per-phase floor slopes ranged from -1.2231 (phase 4, Z) to
  +1.6732 (phase 2, B), and the two Z phases at the extremes of that range are
  +0.4558 and -1.2231 — same rate, same corpus, 75 minutes apart, 1.68 MiB/min
  of disagreement. #105 fitted ONE slope over ONE window and got a CI of
  ±0.12 MiB/min. That CI was honest about its own residuals and silent about
  the fact that a different hour would have given a different answer. This run
  can measure that, and the answer is that an hour-long fleet-RSS floor slope on
  this machine has a replicate-to-replicate spread of roughly ±0.7 MiB/min.
  Any single-window slope smaller than that — including #105's +1.23 — is
  inside the noise this design just measured.

  cause=ARENA / UNRETURNED PAGES is P5 and is reported in §6. Its coverage is
  badly compromised and that is stated there, not here.

  THE TWELVE PHASE FITS, which are the 12 points the decomposition is fitted
  through. This table IS the run:

  phase rate  analysed  buckets   floorSlope   floor 95% CI          Theil-Sen   meanSlope  medLoad1
      1    A        33        7      -0.4855   [ -0.9702, -0.0009]     -0.5089     -0.3555    120.33
      2    B       117       12      +1.6732   [ +0.1107, +3.2357]     +1.6203     +1.6269      9.87
      3    Z        43       10      +0.4558   [ -0.3908, +1.3025]     +0.3665     +0.7614      8.70
      4    Z       118       12      -1.2231   [ -2.3985, -0.0478]     -0.9079     -1.4119      5.69
      5    A        75        9      +0.2829   [ -0.1416, +0.7074]     -0.0893     -0.3022      4.61
      6    B       118       12      +0.0462   [ -0.9464, +1.0388]     -0.1290     +0.5019      5.89
      7    B        68        8      +0.1144   [ -0.9957, +1.2244]     +0.4060     -0.7850      6.52
      8    Z       102       12      -0.1834   [ -0.8630, +0.4962]     +0.2776     +0.4990      7.94
      9    A       111       12      -0.0170   [ -0.6260, +0.5920]     -0.0771     +0.1457      8.57
     10    Z       115       12      -0.0411   [ -0.0822, +0.0000]     -0.0373     +0.1142      8.31
     11    B       115       12      +0.4779   [ -0.3515, +1.3072]     +0.2629     +0.9265      4.95
     12    A       114       12      +0.2419   [ -0.6024, +1.0861]     -0.0135     +0.2415      5.18

  SENSITIVITY TO THE FLOOR BUCKET WIDTH. Pre-registered at 5 minutes, argued in
  analyze109.ts §3, and structurally the only width any verdict field reads.
  The ticket asked for the width to be pre-registered because #105's post-hoc
  floor moved between +0.66 and +1.13 across four width-by-warm-up combinations.
  judge() prints the table under `sensitivityNotAVerdict`:

      width     A         B         Z
      5m     +0.0056   +0.5779   -0.2479     <- the registered width
      10m    +0.1210   +0.5064   -0.1959
      15m    -0.0545   +0.3502   -0.0931

  The width moves A between -0.05 and +0.12, B between +0.35 and +0.58, and Z
  between -0.25 and -0.09. Every cell is inside its own rate's replicate spread.
  Unlike #105, the width choice here does not change any conclusion — which is
  what pre-registering it was supposed to make checkable, and is now checked.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
6. THE FIVE PRE-REGISTERED PREDICTIONS, ONE BY ONE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

  All five were written down in analyze109.ts §4 before the run, each with the
  number that would count as cache and the number that would count as leak.
  Each is reported here with what was predicted, what was observed, and which
  hypothesis it supports — including the ones that support neither.

  THE SYNTHETIC VALIDATION BAR. Before launch, the harness worker fed the same
  judge() two synthetic series with known ground truth. A pure leak returned
  L = 1.2319 with c's CI spanning zero; a pure cache returned c = 3.99 with L's
  CI spanning zero. Those two numbers are what a clean answer looks like coming
  out of this instrument, and they are the yardstick P1 is measured against.

P1 — RATE SCALING.  VERDICT: NEITHER. Ill-conditioned; supports neither
     hypothesis at the resolution this run achieved.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    predicted, cache:  f_Z ~ 0 and (f_B - f_Z)/(f_A - f_Z) ~ 10
    predicted, leak:   f_Z ~ f_A ~ f_B, ratio ~ 1

    observed:  f_Z = -0.2480   f_A = +0.0056   f_B = +0.5779   ratio = 3.258

    3.258 sits between the two predictions, which by itself would be a STRADDLE.
    It is worse than a straddle: the denominator is f_A - f_Z = +0.2536, and
    both terms have replicate standard deviations (0.353 and 0.705) larger than
    the difference between them. A ratio whose denominator is indistinguishable
    from zero is not a measurement, and the ratio's own value is not stable —
    the 10-minute and 15-minute bucket widths give 3.62 and 4.62 for the same
    quantity. P1 does not discriminate.

    Against the synthetic bar:
      pure leak  gave L = 1.2319, c spanning zero
      pure cache gave c = 3.99,   L spanning zero
      this run   gave L = -0.1634 (CI spans zero) AND
                      c = +0.0025 (CI spans zero, and 1,595x below the
                      synthetic cache's c)
    The real run landed on NEITHER side of that bar. It landed at the origin.
    That is not the instrument failing — the synthetics prove it can find both
    signals when they are present. It is the fleet not producing either signal
    at a magnitude this design can resolve in 60-minute phases.

P2 — THE 150-MINUTE FROZEN BLOCK (phases 3+4, minutes 210-360).
     VERDICT: CACHE-CONSISTENT, and more importantly LEAK-REFUTING.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    predicted, cache:  floor slope flat; 95% CI contains zero
    predicted, leak:   ~ +1.2334 MiB/min (#105's own measured rate), CI
                       excluding zero, i.e. +185 MiB gained across the block

    observed:  floor slope  +0.0461 MiB/min   CI [-0.3078, +0.4001]
               n = 161 analysed samples
               rows at window start  59,654
               rows at window end    59,654   (identical — genuinely frozen)
               observed fleet rise across the block  -2.95 MiB

    The corpus did not move by one row for 150 consecutive minutes and the fleet
    LOST three megabytes. #105's rate, applied here, predicted +185 MiB. The
    95% CI [-0.31, +0.40] excludes +1.2334 by a factor of three on its upper
    bound. This is the single cleanest comparison in the run and it comes out
    unambiguously: over a 150-minute window with nothing arriving, there is no
    sustained growth. Whatever #105 measured, it is not a per-minute leak of
    that size, because a per-minute leak does not care that the traffic stopped.

    This is the one prediction that returned exactly the kind of answer it was
    designed to return, and the ticket's framing — "there is no third thing 185
    MiB could be" — holds. The 185 MiB was not there.

P3 — Z-REPLICATE DECAY.  VERDICT: INCONCLUSIVE, n = 4 and non-monotone.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    predicted, cache-still-filling:  monotone decay, f_Z(3) > f_Z(4) > f_Z(8) >
                                     f_Z(10); Spearman strongly negative
    predicted, leak:                 no trend; Spearman ~ 0

    observed, in occurrence order:
      phase  3  +0.4558
      phase  4  -1.2231
      phase  8  -0.1834
      phase 10  -0.0411
    Spearman rank correlation against order = -0.2

    judge() surfaces this directly under predictions.P3_zeroReplicateDecay; I did
    not need to compute it separately, and re-deriving it would have been a
    second analysis competing with the pre-registered one.

    -0.2 is nearer the leak prediction (~0) than the cache prediction (strongly
    negative), and the sequence is NOT monotone — it falls, then rises twice.
    But with n = 4, a Spearman of -0.2 is one of a handful of achievable values
    and its two-sided p is 1.00. Nothing can be concluded. What the four numbers
    DO show is the spread already noted in §4: four measurements of the same
    quantity under identical conditions ranging over 1.68 MiB/min. P3's real
    contribution to this report is that spread, not its rank correlation.

    Honesty about the design: P3 was bought specifically to close the "a cache
    could still be filling against a frozen corpus" loophole on P2. It did not
    close it. It is not able to, at four replicates with this much
    phase-to-phase variance. The loophole stays open and is listed in §11.

P4 — PER-ROW INVARIANCE.  VERDICT: CACHE-CONSISTENT, and it agrees with the
     ORDERING in P1 while inheriting all of P1's imprecision.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    predicted, cache:  g_A ~ g_B, a constant MiB per 1,000 rows
    predicted, leak:   g_B ~ g_A / 10 (the same MiB/min spread over 10x rows)

    observed (pooled over each rate's four phases, using each phase's OBSERVED
    row count, not its nominal rate):
      g_A = +0.0925 MiB per 1,000 rows
      g_B = +0.9632 MiB per 1,000 rows
      g_A / g_B = 0.096

    Read literally this is the LEAK prediction with the arms swapped: the leak
    hypothesis predicted g_B ~ g_A/10, and what was observed is g_A ~ g_B/10.
    Neither pre-registered prediction says that. It arises mechanically because
    f_A came out at +0.0056 — essentially zero — so g_A is zero divided by a
    row count, and the ratio is a statement about a numerator that is noise.

    Per-phase, the same quantity is: A phases -8.09, +4.71, -0.28, +4.03 and
    B phases +2.79, +0.08, +0.19, +0.80 MiB per 1,000 rows. The A column's sign
    changes twice and its magnitude spans 12.8. There is no per-row constant to
    be invariant here.

    P4 was pre-registered as "the same data in the other unit, as a cross-check
    that must agree with P1 or something is wrong with the fit". It does agree
    with P1: both say B > A > Z in central tendency, and both say the spacing
    between them cannot be resolved. The fit is not wrong. The signal is absent.

P5 — WHAT THE MEMORY IS MADE OF.  VERDICT: judge() returns
     cause=ARENA / UNRETURNED PAGES, with pageCache.refuted=false. THE
     PRE-REGISTERED REFUTATION COULD NOT BE EVALUATED AS WRITTEN, because
     3 of the 5 eyes stopped emitting probe readings in the first ten minutes.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    What judge() reports, verbatim from §3:
      cause.verdict                       "ARENA / UNRETURNED PAGES"
      cause.readings                      3209        readingsAnalysed 2337
      cause.pageCache.refuted             false
      cause.pageCache.fleetRiseMiB        216.8
      cause.pageCache.maxPageCacheBoundMiB  7.813   (ceiling 16)
      cause.pageCache.nonDatabaseEyesAlsoRose  false
      cause.pageCache.archiverIsOnlyDatabaseEye  true

    The refutation rule needed BOTH (a) the page-cache upper bound staying small
    — satisfied, 7.813 MiB against a 16 MiB ceiling — AND (b) the four eyes that
    never open a Database rising too. (b) returned false. It returned false
    because three of those four eyes have no data, not because they were
    measured and found flat. Probe readings per eye:

      eye        readings   first          last                    last uptime
      archiver       1588   15:15:58.462Z  2026-08-28T07:16:02.714Z  57604.3 s
      honcho         1587   15:15:58.462Z  2026-08-28T07:15:46.413Z  57588.0 s
      retro            20   15:15:58.461Z  2026-08-27T15:25:30.760Z    572.3 s
      reviewer         10   15:15:58.461Z  2026-08-27T15:20:30.060Z    271.6 s
      digest            4   15:15:58.462Z  2026-08-27T15:17:28.805Z     90.3 s

    memProbe.maybeSample() is called once per iteration of the watcher poll loop
    (src/watch.ts:308). digest, reviewer and retro kept the same pid, stayed
    registered with the supervisor as "running", and never emitted another
    reading after minute 1.5, 4.5 and 9.5 respectively. Their poll loops stopped
    turning. §10 covers the cause; here the consequence is what matters: P5's
    per-eye arm covered 2 of 5 eyes for 99% of the run.

    WHAT THE TWO COVERED EYES SHOW. Slopes over readings past 60 minutes'
    uptime, MiB/min, 95% CI, with the total each implies across 900 minutes:

      archiver (the ONLY eye that opens a Database — src/eyes/archiver/archiver.ts:58)
        rss           -0.00319  [-0.00416, -0.00221]     -2.9 MiB
        heapUsed      -0.00076  [-0.00102, -0.00051]     -0.7 MiB
        external      -0.00052  [-0.00072, -0.00031]     -0.5 MiB
        arrayBuffers  -0.00045  [-0.00064, -0.00027]     -0.4 MiB
        residual      -0.00146  [-0.00254, -0.00038]     -1.3 MiB

      honcho
        rss           +0.01926  [+0.01680, +0.02172]    +17.3 MiB
        heapUsed      +0.00807  [+0.00679, +0.00935]     +7.3 MiB
        external      +0.00352  [+0.00287, +0.00417]     +3.2 MiB
        arrayBuffers  -0.00008  [-0.00016, -0.00000]     -0.1 MiB
        residual      +0.00776  [+0.00425, +0.01126]     +7.0 MiB

    The archiver — the process where a SQLite page cache is the only place it
    could be — SHRANK on every term while the database file it reads grew from
    10.55 MiB to 125.84 MiB:

      elapsed  pageCount   page-cache BOUND   db file      archiver RSS
        60       2,700         7.81 MiB       10.55 MiB      22.50 MiB
       210      10,319         7.81 MiB       40.31 MiB      32.12 MiB
       360      10,319         7.81 MiB       40.31 MiB      25.16 MiB
       510      18,354         7.81 MiB       71.70 MiB      41.98 MiB
       660      23,228         7.81 MiB       90.73 MiB      26.73 MiB
       810      23,962         7.81 MiB       93.60 MiB      26.52 MiB
       959      32,214         7.81 MiB      125.84 MiB      34.20 MiB

    The bound is pinned at 7.81 MiB from minute 8 onward because cache_size is
    2,000 pages and pageCount passed 2,000 in the first ten minutes; after that
    min(cache_size, page_count) is just cache_size. A cache that is capped at
    7.81 MiB cannot be the mechanism behind a 12x growth in corpus, and the
    archiver's RSS did not grow with the corpus in any case. THE "SQLITE PAGE
    CACHE TRACKING A GROWING ARCHIVE" HYPOTHESIS IS DEAD ON THIS EVIDENCE, even
    though judge()'s formal refutation flag reads false for lack of eye
    coverage. One of #105 §7(4)'s three candidates is eliminated. That was the
    stated point of this arm and it is the arm's one clean result.

    honcho's +17.3 MiB over 900 minutes is the only sustained in-process growth
    anywhere in the run, and it is NOT arena-shaped: it splits 7.3 MiB heapUsed
    / 3.2 MiB external / 7.0 MiB residual, i.e. live JS objects and non-heap
    allocations tied to them are growing alongside the residual, not instead of
    it. §10 explains what honcho was doing. judge()'s cause=ARENA verdict is
    computed across the fleet and the archiver's five negative terms dominate
    it; the per-eye split above is a POST-HOC refinement of that label and does
    not overturn it.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
7. THE OVERALL CALL
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

  LEAK — NO. Refuted at #105's own rate, on this run's own strongest evidence.
  P2 is the load-bearing result: 150 minutes, corpus frozen to the row, fleet
  RSS floor slope +0.0461 MiB/min with CI [-0.3078, +0.4001], and a fleet that
  ended the window 2.95 MiB LIGHTER than it started. A leak of +1.2334 MiB/min
  would have put +185 MiB in that window. It is not there, and P2's CI excludes
  that rate three times over. The pre-registered decomposition agrees from the
  other direction: L = -0.1634, CI [-0.6640, +0.3372].

  CACHE — NOT DEMONSTRATED, AND THE ONE MECHANISM NAMED FOR IT IS DEAD.
  c = +0.0025 MiB/min per row-per-sample, CI [-0.0004, +0.0054] — spans zero,
  and 1,595 times below what the synthetic pure-cache validation produced. The
  ordering B > A > Z appears in both P1 and P4, which is the direction a cache
  would push, but it does not clear its own replicate spread in either. And P5
  kills the specific mechanism #105 §7(4) proposed: the archiver's page cache is
  hard-capped at 7.81 MiB by cache_size=2000, and the archiver's own RSS fell
  while the corpus grew 12x.

  SO: leakVsCache=NEITHER, and I am reporting it as NEITHER rather than
  dressing it as a weak cache. The fleet did not grow.

  DO P1-P5 DISAGREE? Not with each other — with the PREMISE.
    P1 supports neither hypothesis (ratio 3.258, denominator ~ 0).
    P2 refutes leak, is consistent with cache-or-nothing.
    P3 is inconclusive at n = 4.
    P4 supports cache in ordering, supports nothing in magnitude.
    P5 refutes the specific cache mechanism, and finds the only real in-process
       growth in the one eye that was in a degraded error loop.
  Every one of them is consistent with a sixth explanation the pre-registration
  did not enumerate: THERE WAS NOTHING TO DECOMPOSE. The design assumed #105's
  +1.2334 MiB/min was real and asked what it was made of. It did not reproduce.
  A crossover that varies the rate cannot attribute growth that is not occurring,
  and that is what happened here.

  THE HONEST FORM OF THE RESULT: #109 did not find that #105's growth was a
  cache. #109 found that #105's growth DID NOT RECUR over sixteen hours at
  #105's own rate, at ten times that rate, and at zero. The most likely reading
  — flagged as an interpretation, not a measurement — is that #105's +1.23
  MiB/min was a 180-minute window drawn from a distribution whose hour-to-hour
  replicate spread this run measures at roughly ±0.7 MiB/min, on a machine whose
  load1 fell monotonically across that same window. #105 said as much in its own
  §7 Test 2 and could not test it. This run tests it, twelve times, and finds no
  underlying rate for those replicates to be scattered around.

  WHAT WOULD OVERTURN THIS. A single Z phase — 60 analysed minutes, corpus
  frozen — with a floor slope whose CI excludes zero and sits above +0.5. Four
  were run. None did. That is the falsification this design offered, and it
  survived.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
8. SEARCH LATENCY UNDER PHASED TRAFFIC
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

  THE PRE-REGISTERED VERDICT, produced by latency.ts UNMODIFIED and handed back
  by latency109.ts under `preRegistered` (full JSON in §5):

    provenance cursor = GREW WITH LOAD, NOT CORPUS
    soak traffic      = WITHIN BUDGET
    sqlite backlog    = WITHIN BUDGET
    backstop          = BREACHED(>=5000ms)

    query              bar    OLS    Theil-Sen   95% CI            r(rows) r(load)
    provenance cursor   —    1.4574   2.0492   [0.7599, 2.1549]     0.136   0.272
    soak traffic       5.2   2.3414   2.2959   [2.0590, 2.6238]     0.269   0.179
    sqlite backlog     4.7   2.0815   2.0905   [1.7985, 2.3645]     0.160   0.202

  DOES #110's RATE-INVARIANT CONSTANT HOLD UNDER PHASED TRAFFIC? YES, and this
  is a strong out-of-sample confirmation rather than a weak one.

  #110 fitted its constants at 21,066 rows on a single-rate run. This run ended
  at 190,452 rows — NINE TIMES out of sample — with the corpus arriving at three
  different rates including a dead stop, four times.

    query              #110 measured   #109 measured    bar    inside?
    soak traffic          2.1542          2.3414        5.2    yes, 2.2x margin
    sqlite backlog        1.5182          2.0815        4.7    yes, 2.3x margin
    provenance cursor     0.4041          1.4574         —     flat-class, held

  Both barred constants moved UP but stayed well inside bars set at 2x #105's CI
  upper bound, and the CIs of the two runs overlap on `soak traffic`
  ([2.059, 2.624] here against #105's upper bound 2.584). The constant is stable
  across a 9x corpus and across a 10x swing in arrival rate. ADR-0003's budget
  survives.

  `provenance cursor` returned its pre-registered flat-class verdict GREW WITH
  LOAD, NOT CORPUS: r(load) = 0.272 exceeds r(rows) = 0.136, exactly the shape
  #110 registered for it (#105 had -0.070 and +0.394).

  THE CONSTANT ESTIMATED SEPARATELY AT EACH RATE (measurements; no bar was
  pre-registered per rate, and latency109.ts's docblock forbids inventing one):

    query              A (30 rows/sample)          B (300 rows/sample)     #110
    soak traffic       2.3776 [1.8786, 2.8766]   2.3535 [1.5404, 3.1667]  2.1542
    sqlite backlog     2.0529 [1.5371, 2.5687]   2.2043 [1.3869, 3.0216]  1.5182
    provenance cursor  0.5912 [-1.2857, 2.4680]  2.2114 [1.4316, 2.9912]  0.4041

  A and B agree to within 1% on `soak traffic` (2.3776 vs 2.3535) and within 7%
  on `sqlite backlog`, with heavily overlapping CIs in both cases. That is the
  per-archived-row framing being VINDICATED, not hidden: the cost per row does
  not depend on how fast the rows arrive. This is the cleanest test of ADR-0003's
  central assumption anyone has run, and it passes.

  `provenance cursor` is the exception — 0.59 at rate A against 2.21 at rate B —
  and it is the one query pre-registered as flat-class precisely because its
  latency is driven by machine load rather than corpus. Its A-rate CI spans zero.
  Nothing follows from the gap except that a load-driven query gives different
  answers in windows with different loads, which is why it has no bar.

  FROZEN-CORPUS DRIFT — the thing #105 could never test, because its corpus
  never stopped growing. Fitted against TIME inside each Z phase, per phase
  (never pooled, because pooling four separated windows would read the A and B
  traffic between them as drift):

    query = soak traffic (the broad one)
      phase  3   +0.0912 ms/min  [-0.5493, +0.7317]  n= 43  median 119.4 ms
      phase  4   +0.3733 ms/min  [+0.1370, +0.6095]  n=118  median 119.4 ms
      phase  8   +8.5034 ms/min  [-14.5202, +31.527] n=102  median 306.7 ms
      phase 10   -0.2150 ms/min  [-1.3324, +0.9024]  n=115  median 319.1 ms

    query = sqlite backlog
      phase  3   +1.0236  [-1.2043, +3.2515]     phase  4  +0.1713 [-0.2089, +0.5515]
      phase  8  +15.3333  [-23.0283, +53.6948]   phase 10  -0.0097 [-0.8517, +0.8323]

    query = provenance cursor
      phase  3   -0.1095  [-1.3817, +1.1626]     phase  4  +0.4679 [+0.2299, +0.7058]
      phase  8  +18.1272  [-20.8796, +57.1339]   phase 10  +0.0112 [-1.3319, +1.3544]

  Twelve fits. TEN have CIs containing zero. Two exclude zero, both in phase 4,
  both small: +0.3733 and +0.4679 ms/min. Phase 8's three fits have CIs 40-78 ms
  wide and are not a measurement of anything — that window had a load1 excursion
  to 98 at its start and a second to 23.6 in its 630-645 minute bucket, and
  within phase 8 the correlation between `soak traffic` latency and load1 is
  +0.685. It is load, and phase 8's point estimates should be read as noise.

  Phase 10 is the clean one: 115 samples, corpus frozen at 141,155 rows, load1
  median 8.3, and all three queries returning drift indistinguishable from zero
  (-0.2150, -0.0097, +0.0112 ms/min). Over a 60-minute window with the corpus
  held exactly still, search did not get slower.

  SO: SEARCH DOES NOT GET SLOWER FROM PROCESS AGE OR FRAGMENTATION ALONE. Held
  against a frozen corpus, in the two Z phases where load was quiet, the drift is
  zero to within a millisecond per minute. Corpus size, not uptime, is what costs
  — which is the positive result latency109.ts's docblock said a flat Z-phase fit
  would be, and it is the assumption ADR-0003's per-row constant rests on. It has
  now been checked rather than assumed.

  THE BACKSTOP BREACH, accounted for rather than left as a scary string. The
  verdict line ends `backstop=BREACHED(>=5000ms)`. The breaches are:

    provenance cursor   2 samples of 1539 (0.13%), at elapsed 643.2 and 644.5
    sqlite backlog      2 samples of 1539 (0.13%), at elapsed 643.2 and 644.5
    soak traffic        1 sample  of 1539 (0.06%), at elapsed 643.2
    union               2 DISTINCT SAMPLES in the entire sixteen hours

  Both are in the same 78-second span. load1 at those two samples was 290.9 and
  177.4, against a run median of 6.70 — 43x and 26x the typical load. Maxima were
  31.1 s, 19.5 s and 32.1 s. The corpus at that moment was 136,763 rows and
  FROZEN (phase 8, Z), so corpus growth cannot be the explanation; the machine
  was. #105's own §9 recorded the same pattern. Two samples out of 1,539 at 40x
  load is a load excursion, not a latency regression, and the pre-registered
  per-query verdicts — which are the ones with bars — all returned WITHIN BUDGET
  or the registered flat-class result.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
9. RESTART, WAL AND TEARDOWN ACCOUNTING — FULL 16 HOURS
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

9a. RESTARTS: ZERO. Confirmed four independent ways, not eyeballed.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    1. `restartLines` was 0 at EVERY ONE of the 1,540 samples. The distinct set
       of values that field took across the run is exactly {0}.
    2. judge().restarts.pidChanges = [] and supervisorLogLines = 0.
    3. Each eye held ONE pid for the whole run, in both series.jsonl and
       memprobe.jsonl: archiver 37276, digest 37278, honcho 37277, retro 37279,
       reviewer 37275. memprobe.jsonl contains exactly one distinct pid per eye
       across all 3,209 readings.
    4. supervisor.log (33,083 lines, 2.9 MB) contains ZERO restart, respawn,
       exit, crash or ceiling lines. Every line in it is one of five shapes:
         24,791  argus watch honcho: recoverable error: Honcho cannot route
                 agent-authored message for 'unknown'
          8,288  Honcho eye flush stopped: eye retry stopped by daemon shutdown
              1  HONCHO DEGRADED: credentials unavailable; messages remain
                 queued and the eye keeps retrying
              1  argus watch reviewer: recoverable error: eye retry stopped by
                 daemon shutdown
              1  argus watch retro:    (same)
              1  argus watch digest:   (same)

    The 512 MiB ceiling was never approached, let alone breached: peak fleet RSS
    was 393.64 MiB across FIVE processes, and the largest single eye ever
    observed was 158.6 MiB (digest). #86's 2.2-2.45 GiB per eye is three orders
    of magnitude away.

9b. WAL: HEALTHY AT ALL THREE RATES, AND UNAFFECTED BY THE 10x STEP.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  phase rate  observed rows/min   WAL median   WAL max    WAL at phase end   shm
      1    A               13.9       2.00 MB    4.16 MB            0.34 MB     32 KiB
      2    B              595.2       2.39 MB    4.01 MB            0.11 MB     32 KiB
      3    Z                0.0       2.22 MB    2.22 MB            2.22 MB     32 KiB
      4    Z                0.0       2.22 MB    2.22 MB            2.22 MB     32 KiB
      5    A               37.5       2.14 MB    4.03 MB            1.19 MB     32 KiB
      6    B              598.0       1.95 MB    4.29 MB            0.40 MB     32 KiB
      7    B              550.3       1.08 MB    4.15 MB            1.19 MB     32 KiB
      8    Z                0.0       1.33 MB    1.33 MB            1.33 MB     32 KiB
      9    A               58.2       2.04 MB    4.31 MB            3.82 MB     32 KiB
     10    Z                0.0       0.22 MB    0.22 MB            0.22 MB     32 KiB
     11    B              597.6       1.93 MB    4.24 MB            4.03 MB     32 KiB
     12    A               59.8       2.23 MB    4.16 MB            2.64 MB     32 KiB

    Run-wide: walMaxBytes 4,313,672 (4.31 MB); walFinalBytes 2,640,952 (2.64 MB);
    shm 32,768 bytes on every single sample; freelistCount 0 or 1 throughout;
    archive.db grew 8.49 MB -> 131.93 MB.

    THE QUESTION THE TICKET ASKS — does checkpointing behave differently when
    the rate jumps 10x at a phase boundary? NO, and the reason is visible in the
    numbers. SQLite's default wal_autocheckpoint is 1,000 pages, which at
    pageSize 4,096 is 4.096 MB. The WAL maximum in EVERY traffic-bearing phase
    lands at 4.0-4.3 MB regardless of rate:

      A phases:  4.16, 4.03, 4.31, 4.16 MB
      B phases:  4.01, 4.29, 4.15, 4.24 MB

    Ten times the write rate produces the SAME ceiling, reached ten times more
    often. That is the autocheckpoint threshold doing exactly its job: the WAL
    is bounded by a page count, not by a time or a rate, so rate changes the
    frequency of checkpoints and not their trigger point. There is no
    accumulation across a boundary and no phase in which the WAL failed to come
    back down — every A and B phase ends between 0.11 and 4.03 MB.

    Z PHASES ARE THE CONTROL AND THEY BEHAVE EXACTLY AS THEY MUST: the WAL is
    CONSTANT across all four (median = max = final: 2.22, 2.22, 1.33, 0.22 MB).
    Zero writes, zero WAL movement, for 240 analysed minutes. That is a direct
    confirmation that the traffic manipulation reached the database layer and not
    just the log writer.

    The one thing worth flagging for a future run: the WAL is never checkpointed
    to zero at a phase boundary, so each Z phase inherits whatever the preceding
    phase left (2.22, 2.22, 1.33, 0.22 MB) and holds it. Harmless at these sizes;
    it means "WAL at start of Z" is a property of the previous phase, not of Z.

9c. TEARDOWN: THE SAME DOUBLE-FIRE AS #105, AND IT CONVERGED.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    Verbatim from teardown.log:

      07:16:09Z teardown start (reason: driver-trap)
      07:16:09Z TERM supervisor pid 37256
      07:16:09Z traffic pid 37260 already gone
      07:16:09Z TERM sampler pid 37261
      07:16:09Z TERM watcher pid 37276 / 37278 / 37277 / 37279 / 37275
      07:16:09Z teardown start (reason: stop-file)
      07:16:09Z TERM supervisor pid 37256
      07:16:09Z traffic pid 37260 already gone
      07:16:09Z sampler pid 37261 already gone
      07:16:09Z TERM watcher pid 37276 / 37278 / 37277 / 37279 / 37275
      07:16:19Z KILL leftover pid 37262: /bin/bash .../scripts/soak/reap.sh
                /private/tmp/argus-soak-5oAcZD stop-file
      07:16:19Z teardown done; survivors=0

    SAME SHAPE AS #105, and #105 already derived the mechanism, so it is not
    re-derived here: the driver's EXIT trap fires teardown, and the STOP file the
    driver drops on its way out fires the watchdog's teardown a moment later.
    Both paths are idempotent by construction — the second pass finds traffic and
    sampler "already gone" and re-TERMs the five watchers harmlessly. R4 (a
    teardown in a trap that survives the driver) is what makes the double-fire
    possible and is also what makes it safe.

    The ONE difference from #105 worth naming: the "leftover pid" killed at
    +10 s here is 37262, which driver.log identifies as the WATCHDOG
    (reap.sh, "watchdog pid 37262 hard deadline 2026-08-28T07:35:58Z"). It is
    leftover because it is the process that fired the second teardown — it was
    still alive because it was mid-teardown. That is self-consistent, not a new
    leak, and it needed no investigation beyond reading the two log lines that
    name the same pid.

    Convergence: survivors=0, and independently confirmed live in §12 below —
    0 `cli.ts watch` processes, 0 launchd argus entries, hours after the fact.

    R3 was honoured throughout: nothing in this accounting judges an orphan by
    ppid. The checks are RSS growth (§4, §6) and launchctl registration (§12).
    R5 was honoured: exactly one fleet, sequential phases, never two.
    R6 was honoured: no cmux workspace was created at any point.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
10. THE TWO INSTRUMENT FAILURES, AND THE DISPATCHER'S EYEBALL CORRECTED
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

10a. THREE OF FIVE EYES STOPPED TURNING THEIR POLL LOOP IN THE FIRST TEN MINUTES.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    This is the most important limitation of the run and it belongs above the
    verdict, not in a footnote. digest, reviewer and retro emitted their last
    memprobe reading at 90.3 s, 271.6 s and 572.3 s of uptime respectively, and
    then nothing for the remaining ~15.8 hours. They did NOT die: same pid,
    supervisor-status.json lists all five as "running" at 07:16:08Z, and their
    external RSS kept being sampled by `ps` every 30 s until teardown.
    `memProbe?.maybeSample()` is the first statement in the watcher poll loop
    body (src/watch.ts:308), so no readings means no iterations.

    THE LIKELY CAUSE, stated as a hypothesis and not as a finding, because this
    run cannot prove it: this soak executed the c9175e0 tree. Ticket #114 —
    whose fixes landed on main AFTER this run, as 80edc85 "bound each codex brain
    attempt" and b5855cb "bound every watcher brain attempt at 120s" — is
    precisely the diagnosis that a watcher's brain spawn was UNBOUNDED and could
    park the poll loop indefinitely. digest, reviewer and retro are brain-calling
    eyes; archiver and honcho are the two that kept turning. The timing, the
    population and the mechanism all line up. Confirming it would need a re-run
    on a post-#114 tree, which is §11's first item.

    WHAT IT COSTS THIS REPORT:
      - P5's per-eye arm covered 2 of 5 eyes. Stated in §6.
      - The rate manipulation reached the archiver (which ingests) and the
        traffic generator, and the Z/A/B row counts in §1 prove it reached the
        DATABASE. But three eyes were not consuming the traffic they were meant
        to consume, so the fleet's RSS response to rate is the response of a
        two-eye working fleet plus three parked processes.
      - It does NOT invalidate P2. P2 asks whether fleet RSS rises when nothing
        arrives. Three parked processes make that question EASIER to answer in
        the negative, so P2's leak-refutation is weaker evidence about a busy
        fleet than it looks. That caveat is carried into §11 explicitly.
      - It does NOT explain the absence of #105's slope by itself, because
        #105's five eyes ran the same code and #105's own three brain-calling
        eyes were subject to the same hazard.

    Externally measured, the three parked eyes contributed no sustained growth:
    over the whole run past their last probe, digest -0.00020 MiB/min
    [-0.00433, +0.00393], reviewer +0.00091 [-0.00287, +0.00469], retro -0.00021
    [-0.00153, +0.00111]. All three bounce over a 10-160 MiB range (the macOS
    compressor, §4's r(load1) = -0.2212) with no trend.

    And it explains honcho, the only eye with real growth. honcho was DEGRADED
    from the first second — "credentials unavailable; messages remain queued and
    the eye keeps retrying" — and then logged 24,791 routing failures in sixteen
    hours, about 26 per minute. Its +17.3 MiB over 900 minutes (heapUsed +7.3,
    external +3.2, residual +7.0) is growth in a hot retry-and-queue path in a
    fake HOME with no credentials. That is a real, small, sustained in-process
    growth, and it is an artefact of the harness environment rather than a
    property of a healthy fleet. It should not be promoted to a leak finding on
    this evidence, and it should not be forgotten either — it is §11's item 6.

10b. THE SAMPLER LOST 380 OF 1,920 PLANNED SAMPLES.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    1,540 samples over 959.833 minutes is a mean interval of 37.4 s against a
    planned 30 s. The median interval was 31.0 s — the cadence was fine almost
    always — and the loss is concentrated in eight excursions:

      gap 27.14 min at elapsed  92.3   load1 284.7 -> 316.9
      gap 43.37 min at elapsed 561.5   load1  26.0 -> 98.3
      gap 22.39 min at elapsed 411.4   load1   4.3 -> 53.7
      gap 16.27 min at elapsed 227.4   load1  17.7 -> 14.5
      gap  9.83 min at elapsed  75.2   load1  35.5 -> 39.6
      gap  8.12 min at elapsed 265.1   load1  20.9 -> 17.2
      gap  5.44 min at elapsed  54.2   load1  47.4 -> 41.7
      gap  5.02 min at elapsed 221.9   load1   8.8 ->  7.4

    Every one of the four largest is bracketed by a load1 far above the run's
    median of 6.70. This is the load-reaping the machine does under pressure, and
    it is why phase 1 has 33 analysed samples instead of 120 (load1 median 120.3
    in that window), phase 7 has 68, phase 3 has 43, and phase 5 has 75. Six of
    twelve phases got their full ~115-118.

    The 43-minute hole at elapsed 561.5-604.9 also pushed the DRIVER's own phase
    switch: driver.log shows phase 8 starting at 01:20:55Z, 20 minutes after its
    nominal 01:00:58Z. The ANALYSIS uses nominal boundaries, so it is worth
    confirming this did not mislabel B traffic as Z — it did not. Phase 8's
    analysed window begins at elapsed 604.88, by which point rows had already
    settled at 136,763, and rows was 136,763 at every one of its 102 analysed
    samples. The Z window is genuinely frozen. Phase 7 lost the tail of its own
    window instead, which is why its analysed rate reads 550.3 rather than ~597.

    Cost: fewer samples, wider CIs, and a phase 1 whose 33 samples and 7 buckets
    make its -0.4855 floor slope the least reliable of the twelve. No sample was
    corrupted (sampleErrors = 0, missingRss = 0, eyeCount = 5 on all but the
    first sample); they are missing, not wrong.

10c. THE DISPATCHER'S iter184 OBSERVATION: CONFIRMED IN ITS NUMBERS, CORRECTED
     IN ITS IMPLICATION.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    While monitoring, the dispatcher eyeballed raw totalRssMiB across the start
    of the zero-traffic block — phases 3+4, first 131 samples — and reported "no
    obvious rise, min 111.6 / max 302.0, bouncing", explicitly flagging it as not
    a real analysis (no bucketing, no settling-window discard, no OLS).

    THE NUMBERS ARE EXACTLY RIGHT. Those 131 samples span elapsed 210.2-319.2
    minutes; raw totalRssMiB min 111.6, max 301.9, median 205.2. "Bouncing" is a
    fair description of a series with a 190 MiB peak-to-trough range.

    THE IMPLICATION IS NOT SAFE, AND IN THE UNFORGIVING DIRECTION. A plain OLS on
    exactly those 131 raw samples gives +0.6146 MiB/min, CI [+0.3849, +0.8444] —
    positive, excluding zero, and half of #105's headline rate. An eyeball that
    says "no obvious rise" would have MISSED a slope that a naive fit calls
    significant. That the eyeball's conclusion happens to match the final verdict
    is luck, not method.

    THE PRE-REGISTERED ANALYSIS OVER THE SAME BLOCK — the full 161 analysed
    samples of phases 3+4, 5-minute floor buckets, settling allowance applied —
    returns +0.0461 MiB/min, CI [-0.3078, +0.4001]. FLAT.

    So the pre-registered method and the eyeball agree on the ANSWER while
    disagreeing about everything in between, and the difference between +0.6146
    and +0.0461 is the whole argument for the floor metric: the raw mean over a
    window whose load1 fell from ~26 to ~6 is tracking the macOS compressor
    releasing pages back, and the trough is not. This is the same correction
    #105's collector applied to that run's dispatcher-reported floor number, in
    the same direction and for the same reason. The dispatcher was right to call
    its own reading "explicitly NOT a real analysis"; it was not one, and had the
    fleet been growing, it would not have caught it.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
11. WHAT THIS SOAK DID NOT ESTABLISH
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

  #105's limits section listed what a 3-hour single-rate run could not reach.
  Duration and rate variation were exactly what it lacked, and this run supplied
  both. The limits are therefore DIFFERENT limits, not the same ones with better
  numbers. Twelve of them.

  1. IT DID NOT WATCH A HEALTHY FLEET. Three of five eyes stopped executing their
     poll loop within ten minutes and stayed parked for 15.8 hours (§10a). Every
     fleet-level number in this file — the rss slope, L, c, all four Z blocks,
     P1 through P4 — describes a fleet of two working eyes and three parked
     processes. This is the single largest caveat and it invalidates nothing
     while limiting everything. THE RUN SHOULD BE REPEATED ON A POST-#114 TREE
     (b5855cb or later) BEFORE ITS NEGATIVE RESULT IS TREATED AS SETTLED.

  2. IT DID NOT REPRODUCE #105, SO IT DID NOT EXPLAIN #105. The design assumed
     the +1.2334 MiB/min was there to be decomposed. It was not. This run
     therefore says nothing about what #105 measured except that it did not
     recur — which is a different and weaker claim than "it was a cache".

  3. IT DID NOT CLOSE THE "CACHE STILL FILLING" LOOPHOLE ON P2. That is what P3
     was bought for and P3 came back inconclusive at n = 4 with a Spearman of
     -0.2 and p = 1.00 (§6). A cache that is still filling against a frozen
     corpus remains formally indistinguishable from a slow leak on this data.
     Closing it needs more Z replicates or much longer ones.

  4. IT WAS NOT A QUIET MACHINE, AND THE TICKET ASKED FOR ONE. load1 ranged 2.73
     to 415.23, mean 17.19, p90 37.02. #105's range was 3.2 to 228, mean 21.3.
     This is not better; it is the same machine with a longer tail. The confound
     is smaller here only because the RSS signal is smaller — r(totalRss, load1)
     = -0.2212, against #105's -0.4005 — not because the machine was calmer. A
     genuinely quiet machine is still owed, and this run cost 380 samples and one
     20-minute phase-boundary slip to not having one.

  5. IT DID NOT RUN PAST MINUTE 960. The series ends at 959.833 and stops
     cleanly. There is no evidence about hour 17 or hour 40. #105's convex bend
     at minute 150 is now known not to continue — phases at minutes 210-360 and
     735-810 are flat — but a slower bend at, say, minute 1,500 is simply not
     addressed. The 2.5 MiB/min bar's own justification is about reaching 512 MiB
     "inside a day"; 16 hours is most of a day, not a week.

  6. IT DID NOT DECIDE WHETHER honcho'S GROWTH MATTERS. +17.3 MiB per 900
     minutes, CI [+0.0168, +0.0217] MiB/min — the only sustained in-process
     growth measured anywhere — occurred in an eye that was credential-degraded
     from second one and logged 24,791 routing failures. Whether a
     CREDENTIALLED honcho grows the same way is untested and untestable in this
     harness, which uses a fake HOME on purpose. It is 1/144th of the 2.5
     MiB/min bar, so it is not urgent; it is also the only thing here shaped like
     a leak, so it should not be dropped.

  7. IT DID NOT MEASURE SQLITE'S ACTUAL CACHE OCCUPANCY. `bun:sqlite` exposes no
     sqlite3_memory_used() or sqlite3_db_status(), as memprobe.ts's docblock
     states. What was measured is an UPPER BOUND, min(cache_size, page_count) *
     page_size, pinned at 7.81 MiB from minute 8. A bound refutes; it cannot
     confirm. The page-cache hypothesis is dead (§6 P5) but the positive
     statement "the archiver's cache occupied X MiB" was never available.

  8. IT DID NOT TEST A REPO OR SCALE DIMENSION AT ALL — and neither soak has.
     Both runs watched ONE synthetic repo, seeded from one 5 MB transcript
     corpus, with one 18-word vocabulary, enrolled once. Nothing here speaks to
     N enrolled repos, to a repo with a large git history, to transcripts with
     different shapes, or to a fleet whose eyes are watching different repos.
     190,452 rows is a scale result on ONE axis. The multi-repo axis is
     completely untouched by #105 and #109 alike, and it is the axis a real
     deployment moves along first.

  9. IT DID NOT VARY ANYTHING BUT THE RATE. Poll interval (250 ms), ceiling
     (512 MiB), eye set, cache_size (2,000 pages), and the traffic message text
     were all fixed. cache_size in particular is now known to bind at 7.81 MiB;
     a fleet configured with a large cache_size might behave completely
     differently and this run says nothing about it.

 10. THE PHASE-LEVEL FLOOR SLOPE IS NOISIER THAN THE DESIGN ASSUMED. Replicate
     standard deviations were 0.35 (A), 0.75 (B), 0.71 (Z) MiB/min. The design
     bought 60 analysed minutes per phase on the reasoning that 12 buckets is the
     count this harness has most experience fitting. It is not enough to resolve
     effects below ~0.7 MiB/min, which means this run could not have detected a
     leak at half #105's rate even if one existed. A future design needs longer
     phases, more replicates, or both — and now has a measured variance to size
     them with, which is the most transferable thing this run produced.

 11. P1's RATIO STATISTIC IS STRUCTURALLY FRAGILE AND SHOULD BE REPLACED.
     (f_B - f_Z)/(f_A - f_Z) has a denominator that goes to zero exactly when the
     answer is "no effect", which is the answer that occurred. It returned 3.258
     here, 3.62 at a 10-minute bucket and 4.62 at 15 — a "statistic" that moves
     by 42% with a nuisance parameter. The regression coefficients L and c are
     the sound version of the same test and should be the pre-registered
     discriminator next time, with the ratio demoted to a reported quantity.

 12. IT DID NOT TEST THE 2.5 MiB/min BAR. Nothing came within 1/190th of it. The
     bar remains justified by #86 and unexercised by evidence. Both soaks have
     now returned slopes far below it; neither has produced a value near it, so
     its calibration is still an argument rather than a measurement.

13. RAW SERIES — ALL 1,540 SAMPLES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

  Tab-separated. Columns, and what each is:

    elapsedMin    minutes since the sampler's first tick, as written by the
                  sampler (3 dp)
    phase         phase label at the time the sample was taken ("warmup", 1-12)
    rate          that phase's pre-registered rate class, A / B / Z
    rows          COUNT(*) of the archive at that instant; empty before the
                  archiver opened the database
    totalRssMiB   sum of the five eyes' RSS
    archiver .. reviewer   per-eye RSS, MiB
    load1         1-minute load average
    walB shmB     -wal and -shm file sizes, bytes
    archiveB      archive.db file size, bytes
    pageCount     PRAGMA page_count
    provMs soakMs sqlMs   the three registered query latencies, ms
    restartLines  cumulative supervisor restart lines seen (0 throughout)

  Reconstruction: this is a projection of series.jsonl, which remains at
  /private/tmp/argus-soak-5oAcZD/series.jsonl with
  sha256 37a8664e68a5f257cb59f45932f074b9c0861483eefe2fa9a707a938c7518485.
  The fields dropped by the projection are enumerated in §2 and are all either
  constant across the run or exactly reconstructible from what is kept.
